*Introduced by*: 260720-zx4i-board-route-pane-host-panels

### Window-switch feedback stays optimistic
**Decision**: window-switch navigation stays optimistic — the heading/URL flip at click (acknowledged intent) and the sidebar highlight stays SSE-derived (confirmation); the slide plays only on confirmed-fast arrival, with a pending mask + failure bounce-back layered on.
**Why**: keeps the terminal rendering immediately while giving each visual state an honest, distinct signal.
**Rejected**: pessimistic navigation (waits for confirmation before flipping); a dimmed overlay.
*Introduced by*: 260715-38kg-window-switch-confirmed-motion

### Single theme-color meta writer module
**Decision**: All `meta[name="theme-color"]` writes funnel through one module (`src/instance-accent.ts`) holding module-level `currentAccentHex` + `lastBackground` state; `theme-context.tsx` (`applyThemeColorMeta`) and the instance-accent provider (`setAccentThemeColor`) both write through it, and the content resolves to the accent hex when set, else the theme background.
**Why**: React passive effects run child-first, so `ThemeProvider`'s `applyThemeToDOM` effect fires AFTER any child accent effect on a theme switch and would clobber an accent-tinted meta tag with the bare background. One writer with shared state removes the ordering race at its root rather than papering over it.
**Rejected**: having the accent provider re-write the meta in its own effect after theme changes — loses deterministically to the parent effect's ordering (fixes the symptom, not the cause).
*Introduced by*: 260721-1etw-instance-accent-host-color

### Instance-accent echo carries the precomputed meta hex, not just the descriptor
**Decision**: `runkit-instance-color` stores `{"value","hex"}` where `hex` is the final computed theme-color meta content; the `index.html` blocking pre-paint script applies `hex` verbatim. The echo is a paint cache only — never authoritative (the runtime resolution overwrites it every load).
**Why**: deriving a hex from a descriptor needs the OKLCH/palette machinery (`colorValueToHex` + contrast guard), far too heavy to inline in a blocking script; precomputing at echo time keeps the script a validate-and-apply read. A cross-theme-mode load shows a transient mismatch that self-corrects post-fetch (accepted).
**Rejected**: inlining the derivation in `index.html` (duplicates themes.ts logic in unlintable inline JS); echoing only the descriptor (leaves the script unable to tint).
*Introduced by*: 260721-1etw-instance-accent-host-color

### PWA titlebar carries a subtle blend, not the full accent hue
**Decision**: The theme-color meta content (and the echo's `hex`) is `titlebarHex` — a `blendHex(src, background, INSTANCE_TITLEBAR_RATIO = 0.35)` accent-into-background blend — not the contrast-guarded `stripeHex`. `stripeHex` stays the 2px top-bar stripe and the HOST hostname tint; `washHex` (~6.5%) stays the top-bar background wash. Three distinct derived hexes, one per surface class.
**Why**: mock parity. The design mock tinted the installed-PWA titlebar with a subtle dark blend of the accent into the theme background and relied on the full-brightness 2px stripe sitting directly below it as the vivid accent line. Painting the meta with the full-hue `stripeHex` made the whole titlebar a loud color band and rendered the stripe invisible — it abutted a titlebar painted the identical hex. The blend restores the tinted-titlebar + bright-line pairing the mock designed. Reuses the existing `blendHex` machinery (theme-aware by construction, no hardcoded hexes); `index.html` needs no change because the pre-paint script applies the echoed `hex` verbatim, so swapping the echoed value retints cold start for free.
**Rejected**: keeping the saturated titlebar and instead removing/relocating the stripe (the mock's whole point is the tinted-titlebar + bright-line pairing); a dynamic manifest / tinted dock icons / Badging API (separate follow-up `260722-eo8e-accent-dock-icon`).
*Introduced by*: 260722-y5c3-instance-accent-titlebar-wash

### `titlebarHex` is exposed from the accent context, not re-derived in the strip
**Decision**: `InstanceAccent` carries a nullable `titlebarHex` alongside `stripeHex`/`washHex`, and the shell titlebar strip reads it from the context. It does not call `deriveAccentHexes(color, theme)` itself, and it does not read the rendered `theme-color` meta.
**Why**: The provider already computes all three hexes for the meta bridge, so a second derivation in the strip would be one formula in two places — and the failure mode of drift is silent (a strip and a PWA titlebar that are subtly different colors for the same accent, on surfaces users rarely see side by side). Exposing the field costs a line in the context value and a mechanical fixture update; the field was nullable-by-construction already.
**Rejected**: A strip-local `deriveAccentHexes` call (duplicate derivation, silent drift); reading `document.querySelector('meta[name=theme-color]').content` (a DOM round-trip for state React already holds, and it inverts the dependency — the meta is an *output* of the accent state); passing the hex down as a prop from `AppLayout` (threads accent state through a layout that otherwise does not care).
*Introduced by*: 260731-ofws-shell-accent-titlebar-dock-badge

### Drag regions are a CSS utility class, not an inline-style cast
**Decision**: `globals.css` defines `.rk-shell-drag { -webkit-app-region: drag; }` and `.rk-shell-no-drag { -webkit-app-region: no-drag; }`; the shell titlebar strip applies them as classes. No component writes `-webkit-app-region` inline.
**Why**: `WebkitAppRegion` is not in React's `CSSProperties`, so an inline style needs an `as CSSProperties` assertion — exactly the type-assertion-over-narrowing pattern the project's code-quality rules forbid. A class sidesteps the type system entirely, is inert in browsers, and lands in the `rk-*` utility vocabulary the codebase already uses for non-Tailwind primitives (`rk-scanlines`, `rk-hazard`, `rk-waiting-seam`). The `no-drag` sibling exists for the same reason as the `drag` one and serves the strip's single interactive island (→ The strip's switcher trigger is the band's only no-drag island).
**Rejected**: `style={{ WebkitAppRegion: "drag" } as CSSProperties}` (the banned assertion); a `<DragRegion>` wrapper component (a component to carry one declaration); augmenting React's `CSSProperties` type globally (a global type change to serve one property in one component).
*Introduced by*: 260731-ofws-shell-accent-titlebar-dock-badge

### The strip's switcher trigger is the band's only no-drag island
**Decision**: The titlebar strip band holds exactly ONE interactive element — the host-switcher trigger and its open menu, carrying `.rk-shell-no-drag`. Everything else in the band is a pure drag surface, and no other chrome is admitted into it. The one-island scope IS the rule: the band's job is drag + host identity, and the switcher is admitted only because it IS that identity ([desktop-shell](/run-kit/desktop-shell.md) § Design Decisions → The titlebar is colored by a page-drawn strip).
**Why**: The strip's centered label *names* the switchable identity, so an inert label is a discoverability dead end for the shell's most identity-defining action — otherwise reachable only through the native menu, the ⌥⌘1–9 accelerators, and the palette, all keyboard-or-menu paths where Constitution V asks for a mouse-secondary one. A single carve-out costs nothing the whole-band drag rule protects: the cost that rules out a titlebar merge is `no-drag` exceptions threaded through *every* control of live chrome, whereas one pill on an otherwise unchanged band leaves the drag surface the whole band minus that pill and the SPA top bar below untouched. It also needs zero new IPC — `servers.list()` and `servers.switch` already serve the palette block.
**Rejected**: Leaving the band inert so switching stays keyboard/menu-only (the discoverability dead end above); merging the top bar into the titlebar so its controls become the chrome (the option the strip decision rejects, at the same cost, over a much larger surface); putting the switcher in the top bar below the strip (the strip's label is where the eye already rests for host identity, and duplicating the host name in two bars breaks the move-don't-copy rule); admitting further chrome into the band on this precedent (the island is scoped to identity, not opened to chrome generally).
*Introduced by*: 260731-4bqi-shell-strip-host-switcher-dropdown

### Host-switcher accelerator knowledge lives in three shapes, not one helper
**Decision**: The mac-vs-other host-switcher accelerator knowledge is intentionally triplicated: `app/desktop/src/menu.ts` holds `MAX_SWITCHER_ACCELERATORS` plus Electron's `Alt+Cmd+{n}` / `Shift+Ctrl+{n}` binding syntax; `components/shortcuts-overlay.tsx` holds the `switcherCaps` segmented keycap array keyed on `"mac"`; and `lib/shell-strip.ts`'s `hostAcceleratorHint` holds the `⌥⌘{n}` / `⇧Ctrl+{n}` display string keyed on `"darwin"`. No shared constant spans them.
**Why**: The three consumers span the Electron main/renderer process boundary — `menu.ts` runs in main and cannot import from the SPA bundle — and each needs a genuinely different *shape*: Electron accelerator syntax, an array of keycap segments for the overlay's rendering vocabulary, and a single glyph string for a menu row. Their platform vocabularies also differ (`"mac"` vs `"darwin"`), following each side's own convention. Extracting a shared source would mean a cross-process package plus a shape-conversion layer for three call sites.
**Rejected**: A shared accelerator module imported by all three (crosses the main/renderer boundary the shell's electron-free-module discipline maintains); deriving the display strings from the Electron syntax at runtime (a parser for three literals); collapsing the overlay's segmented array to a plain string so two of the three could share (loses the keycap rendering the overlay is built on). A future change to the binding tier must update all three deliberately — this entry is the pointer that names them.
*Introduced by*: 260731-4bqi-shell-strip-host-switcher-dropdown

### Shell chrome mounts on `isShell()`; switch affordances gate on list emptiness
**Decision**: The titlebar strip and the badge reporter are *mounted* from `AppLayout` behind `isShell()`. Every affordance that actually *switches* hosts — the shell server-switch palette block and the strip's own switcher trigger inside the mounted band — gates instead on `listShellServers()` resolving a non-empty list (`stripSwitcherEnabled`). The two gates nest: the band mounts on `isShell()`, its trigger appears on the list.
**Why**: The gates answer different questions and both answers are right for their surface. A switch affordance asks "does the `servers` bridge group actually answer?" — an older shell exposes `version`/`platform` without the group, and offering a switch that cannot switch is worse than offering none. Window chrome asks "is this a shell window at all?" — the strip must exist whenever the native titlebar is hidden, even if `servers.list()` fails (the label falls back to `location.hostname`), and the reporter is harmless when the `badge` group is absent (`setShellBadge` resolves `false`). Splitting the strip's mount gate from its trigger gate is what lets both answers hold on one surface: a denied list degrades the band to a static label instead of removing the window's drag surface.
**Rejected**: Gating the strip's *mount* on the `servers` list (a failed or denied list call would remove the window's only drag surface — a bridge hiccup becomes an unusable window); gating the strip's trigger on `isShell()` alone (an older shell would render a chevron whose click can never switch); gating the palette on `isShell()` (the less accurate signal for that surface); a single shared gate for all of it (the surfaces have genuinely different failure semantics).
*Introduced by*: 260731-ofws-shell-accent-titlebar-dock-badge

### Open split-button: last-used primary via localStorage, menu when no preference
**Decision**: the Open control's primary segment re-runs the last-used target (persisted kind-qualified to `localStorage["runkit-open-last-used"]`, the `runkit-*` preference convention); with no stored (or no-longer-available) preference the primary click opens the menu instead, and the chevron always opens the menu.
**Why**: mirrors Conductor's split-button UX and the existing `runkit-terminal-font-size` / `runkit-last-pinned-board` preference pattern — a one-click repeat of the common action with the full list one chevron away; a menu-on-first-use avoids firing an arbitrary default. Kind-qualifying the id (`deeplink:vscode` / `host:vscode`) stops a deeplink and host entry for the same editor from colliding on the shared key.
**Rejected**: a fixed default editor (arbitrary, wrong for most); primary always opens the menu (loses the one-click repeat); an un-qualified last-used id (deeplink/host collision for the same editor).
*Introduced by*: 260722-6d0f-navbar-open-in-app

### Open control owns its data via an invalidatable external store; TopBar props untouched
**Decision**: a `useOpenTargets` hook backed by a module-level external store (health + open-apps fetched once, then static between settings commits) feeds both the TopBar registry entry and the palette builder; no new TopBar props or slot-context fields are added. Freshness comes from an exported `invalidateOpenContext()` invoked at the ONE seam the data changes — the Settings dialog's successful SSH-host commit (§ Settings-commit invalidation seam) — NOT from client polling or per-render refetch.
**Why**: keeps the TopBar prop/slot surface stable (the e2e suite asserts chrome details) and avoids client polling (a code-quality anti-pattern). The open context genuinely only changes at the SSH-host commit, so invalidating exactly there is precise and cheap; everywhere else the once-per-load fetch is correct.
**Rejected**: threading `openTargets` through the slot context (prop churn across app.tsx/TopBar/tests); a per-menu-open or per-render refetch (needless requests, popup latency, and it re-fetches even when nothing changed); client polling for freshness (a code-quality anti-pattern — the SSE stream / event seam is the sanctioned path).
*Introduced by*: 260722-6d0f-navbar-open-in-app; invalidatable store 260723-l317-open-button-ssh-refresh-icon

### Settings-commit invalidation seam refreshes the open context, over polling or per-render refetch
**Decision**: the open context is refreshed by an exported `invalidateOpenContext()` called from the Settings dialog's SSH-host `commit` handler after `await setSSHHost(...)` **succeeds** (success only — a rejected commit leaves the server value unchanged, so it must not invalidate). The store is React's `useSyncExternalStore` over a module-level listener `Set` + `getSnapshot`. Invalidation drops the cache and, when subscribers are mounted, eagerly refetches and notifies (mounted consumers re-render fresh, no reload); with zero subscribers it plain-drops the cache for the next mount.
**Why**: the SSH host is runtime-mutable via the Settings dialog (`POST /api/settings/ssh-host`) and the backend serves the fresh value per `GET /api/health` request, but the frontend cached it once per load — so a saved host only reached editor deeplinks after a full page reload, embedding the stale derived `${sshUser}@${location.hostname}` fallback until then. Invalidating at the single commit seam is the precise fix. `useSyncExternalStore` is React 19's canonical external-store primitive — tear-free snapshots, automatic unsubscription on unmount, no `alive`-flag bookkeeping.
**Rejected**: refetching health on every Open-button render (wasteful — the once-per-load cache is correct everywhere except this one seam); client polling (a code-quality anti-pattern — use the event seam); a hand-rolled subscriber array driving per-consumer `useState` (more code for the same behavior, re-implements what the hook provides); invalidating on a failed `setSSHHost` (the commit failed; the server value is unchanged).
*Introduced by*: 260723-l317-open-button-ssh-refresh-icon

### Epoch guard discards in-flight open-context fetches that predate an invalidation
**Decision**: a module-level `epoch` counter is bumped on every `invalidateOpenContext()` (and `resetOpenTargetsCacheForTest()`); a fetch records the epoch at start and writes `cached` / notifies subscribers / clears `pending` **only** when the epoch still matches at resolve time.
**Why**: two rapid SSH-host commits would otherwise let the first (now-stale) refetch resolve after the second invalidation and resurrect exactly the pre-commit data this change exists to kill.
**Rejected**: ignoring the race (small window, but silently serves the stale value the invalidation dropped); aborting via `AbortController` (heavier — the fetch helpers take no signal).
*Introduced by*: 260723-l317-open-button-ssh-refresh-icon

### Remote client shows deeplinks; the derived `user@hostname` replaces the `RK_SSH_HOST` gate
**Decision**: the deeplink section shows for any remote client (no `RK_SSH_HOST` gate). The effective SSH host resolves as `RK_SSH_HOST` verbatim (alias — no `user@` prefix), else a derived `${sshUser}@${location.hostname}` on remote clients (prefix omitted when `sshUser` is empty), else empty for local.
**Why**: on a tailnet `location.hostname` (the name the browser reached the host by) is itself SSH-reachable, and the one missing ingredient — the SSH username — is derivable server-side (`os/user.Current()`, per constitution X, no new config). Gating on `RK_SSH_HOST` left the entire remote story permanently invisible for zero-config Tailscale users. The accepted trade is "shown ⇒ works on tailnets, errors on tunnels": behind an HTTP-only tunnel the derived host is not SSH-reachable and the editor shows its own connect error rather than the section staying hidden. `RK_SSH_HOST` remains the override for aliases, non-standard users, ports, and keys.
**Rejected**: keeping the `RK_SSH_HOST` gate (permanently-dark feature for the common case); probing reachability of the derived host before showing (guessing; the connect-error trade is accepted); an explicit-disable sentinel (`RK_SSH_HOST=none`) in v1 (deferred — most env loaders can't distinguish empty from unset, and the gate change makes disable rarely needed).
*Introduced by*: 260722-fc3b-ssh-host-fallback-open-logos

### Open-row icons are monochrome `currentColor` inline SVGs, resolved id-then-kind
**Decision**: Open menu rows lead with monochrome `currentColor` inline SVG glyphs (~14px, `aria-hidden`, `data-icon` test seam), resolved by an id map (`vscode`+`code` → VS Code glyph, `cursor`, `windsurf`) then a kind-based generic fallback (editor/terminal/file-manager), then a neutral glyph. Palette rows stay text-only.
**Why**: full-color brand marks would clash with the terminal aesthetic, and `currentColor` lets the existing accent-green hover treatments apply to the glyph for free with no new dependency and no image fetches. The kind fallback keeps unknown/future registry entries rendering; both `vscode` (deeplink id) and `code` (wt's VS Code host id) must map to the same glyph because wt's registry id for VS Code is `code`, not `vscode`.
**Rejected**: full-color brand assets (aesthetic clash, licensing/asset pipeline); asserting raw SVG path data (brittle) or per-glyph `aria-label` (icons are decorative — `aria-hidden` keeps row accessible names clean); adding icons to the command palette (no icon affordance today, separate change).
*Introduced by*: 260722-fc3b-ssh-host-fallback-open-logos

### Mirrored menu rows lead with the in-bar glyph; toggle state stays on the trailing ✓
**Decision**: Every chevron-menu / SplitControl-popover row that mirrors an in-bar icon control leads with that control's glyph as its first child, and the glyph is extracted into a shared component so bar and menu render ONE definition each (`top-bar-icons.tsx`). On the two stateful toggle rows the leading icon is a **static identity variant** that never flips with state; the trailing ✓ remains the sole state marker. `ViewSwitcherMenuRows` is excluded, and the command palette stays text-only.
**Why**: the registry already guaranteed bar↔menu *behavior* can never drift (one ordered source drives both renderings), but the menu mixed icon-bearing Open rows with text-only rows — so users matched rows by reading, never learned the icon↔action mapping, and each new `menuOnly` demotion widened the gap. Sharing one component makes visual parity structural rather than copy-paste, and dedupes the direction-glyph SVGs that `SplitControl` alone inlined twice. Leading-icon-as-identity plus trailing-✓-as-state is the macOS menu convention; a leading icon that flipped with state would make the row carry two competing state signals and defeat the identity read. The View switcher is excluded because its in-bar form is a text pill — there is no glyph to mirror.
**Rejected**: icons on action rows only, toggles left text-only (the mixed look the change exists to remove — rejected in favor of the mock-approved "toggles too" variant); a state-flipping leading icon on toggle rows (duplicates the ✓ and breaks the identity read); iconifying the View-switcher rows (nothing to mirror); inventing new glyphs for menu rows instead of mirroring (the bar↔menu mapping is the entire point).
*Introduced by*: 260801-3q1z-top-bar-menu-leading-icons

### A dedicated `top-bar-icons.tsx`, and per-glyph exact SVG attributes over one uniform wrapper
**Decision**: Host the shared control glyphs in a new sibling module `top-bar-icons.tsx` (not in `open-app-icons.tsx`, not in `top-bar-overflow-menu.tsx`), and give it a local `ControlGlyph` wrapper parameterized by `viewBox`/`strokeWidth`/`join` so each glyph byte-preserves its in-bar original's SVG attributes. Stateful toggles take a variant prop defaulting to the static identity form. The terminal-font glyph is an `aria-hidden` text span, not an SVG.
**Why**: `open-app-icons.tsx` is Open-target-specific (its API takes an `OpenTarget`) and `top-bar-overflow-menu.tsx` hosts row classes and sizing tokens, not glyph definitions — a dedicated module mirrors the `open-app-icons.tsx` shape and imports into `top-bar.tsx` with no cycle. The originals genuinely differ (24-viewBox/strokeWidth-2 for split/close/refresh; 14-viewBox/strokeWidth-1.5 round-caps-only for fixed-width; 14-viewBox/strokeWidth-1.5 caps+joins for autofit), so normalizing them onto one wrapper would visibly change stroke weight in the bar — the refactor's whole premise is zero visual change there. A defaulted variant prop is the minimal seam that lets one definition serve both the state-driven bar form and the static menu form. "Aa" is a text glyph in the bar, so a span is the direct match and keeps `currentColor` behavior.
**Rejected**: extending `open-app-icons.tsx` (couples control glyphs to the OpenTarget resolution API); inlining into `top-bar-overflow-menu.tsx` (mixes glyph definitions into the menu-shell component); reusing that module's fixed 24-viewBox/1.8-stroke `Glyph` (changes every glyph's weight); two separate components per stateful toggle (duplicates the definition the change exists to unify); rendering "Aa" as an SVG (needlessly diverges from the in-bar trigger's text styling).
*Introduced by*: 260801-3q1z-top-bar-menu-leading-icons

### `Open: PR #{n}` is a client-side palette action, not an Open target
**Decision**: The PR palette entry is a standalone `openActions` member built by `buildOpenPrAction` — `window.open` in the viewer's own browser, gated on `currentWindow.prUrl`, terminal route only. It is NOT an `OpenTarget`, so it never reaches `buildOpenTargets`, the top-bar Open split-button, or `OpenMenuRows`, and it registers no keyboard chord.
**Why**: `prUrl` is a public GitHub URL already on the client (derived server-side per constitution X), so a client-side open behaves identically for local and remote viewers with no backend surface at all. Keeping it out of `OpenTarget` preserves the documented palette↔menu mirror, which is a mirror of Open *targets* (folder-in-an-app), a different thing from "open this window's PR". The palette entry alone satisfies §V — the sidebar `PrLinkRow` anchor is pointer-only and stays as the mouse path.
**Rejected**: a host-side open via `POST /api/open`/exec (opens a browser on the host where nobody may be looking, fails headless, adds an exec surface for zero benefit); an `OpenTarget` entry plus a top-bar menu row (breaks the target mirror and puts a per-window action in server-wide chrome); a dedicated keyboard chord (the `Open:` family registers none — palette reachability is the §V path); board/tmux-Server-page variants (no unambiguous current window there).
*Introduced by*: 260727-w2d8-palette-open-pr

### Settings dialog mounts at `AppLayout`, not `AppShell`
**Decision**: The settings dialog + its `SettingsDialogContext` mount once at `AppLayout` (the persistent every-page layer), not `AppShell`. Instance display-name delivery lives in a separate root-mounted `InstanceNameProvider` (in `RootWrapper` beside `InstanceAccentProvider`), not in `SettingsDialogContext`.
**Why**: `AppShell` is server-scoped (assumes non-null `currentServer` throughout `app.tsx`), so mounting there would leave the dialog unavailable on `/board/$name`, which renders inside `AppLayout` without `AppShell` — and board coverage is the whole point of the every-page mount. Three display surfaces plus the dialog editor need one live-updating `{hostname, instanceName}` state — the exact fetch-once + optimistic-set + multi-surface-repaint shape `InstanceAccentProvider` already established; keeping it out of the chrome-only `SettingsDialogContext` avoids mixing chrome state with instance data and keeps the provider above the `RootTopBar` consumers anyway.
**Rejected**: mounting at `AppShell` (dead on boards); migrating all AppShell chrome (Sidebar, create/kill dialogs, palette) to `AppLayout` now (separate backlog `[239r]` — the two-line duplicate palette registration is the accepted concession until then); a module-level name cache (no reactivity path for the dialog's live edit); putting the name pair in `SettingsDialogContext` (mixes chrome and instance data).
*Introduced by*: 260723-o7q8-settings-dialog

### Global chrome lives in the sidebar footer; the top bar keeps page-scoped controls
**Decision**: Theme, Help, the connection dot, and the resting version readout live in the sidebar's footer row (readouts left, actions right); notifications live in the settings dialog. The top-bar right cluster carries only page-scoped controls (view lens, Open, splits, fixed-width, terminal font, autofit, close/kill, UpdateChip, Refresh, chevron). The accepted consequence: any state that unmounts the sidebar — the Host route `/`, a collapsed desktop sidebar (`Cmd+\`), a closed mobile drawer — shows no connection indicator, with no mode-gated fallback dot.
**Why**: the split is by ROLE, not by width — the overflow pyramid already solves space, so a crowded bar was the symptom, not the problem. Mixing "acts on this page" with "acts on the app" made both harder to find, and the footer gear had already established a home for app-global chrome. Losing the dot on sidebar-less surfaces is cheap and reversible (re-adding a mode-gated dot is a small change), and those surfaces are exactly the ones with the least live per-page data to report on.
**Rejected**: keeping the dot in the bar and moving only theme/help (leaves the bar mixing roles for the one item users read as global status); a host-route special-case dot (a second dot implementation to keep in sync for a page whose only live source is host metrics); a settings *page* (constitution §IV — no settings pages).
*Introduced by*: 260724-6j1v-sidebar-footer-chrome-settings

### Connection state reaches the Sidebar as a prop, not through the top-bar slot
**Decision**: `isConnected` is a required `SidebarProps` field — AppShell passes its chat-aware `dotConnected`, BoardPage its `boardConnected`. Neither `TopBarSlot` nor `TopBarProps` carries an `isConnected` field, and the Host page registers no slot at all (connection state was its only payload).
**Why**: explicit dataflow, unit-testable without mounting a slot provider and registering a fake slot, and it leaves no dead slot plumbing behind. The footer dot gets exactly the same per-page values (chat-aware on terminal routes, AND-over-servers on boards).
**Rejected**: the Sidebar reading `useTopBarSlot()` (keeps a dead-ish field alive, couples the sidebar to top-bar plumbing, and forces every sidebar test into a slot-provider harness).
*Introduced by*: 260724-6j1v-sidebar-footer-chrome-settings

### Shared chrome definitions live in `global-chrome.tsx`
**Decision**: `HELP_URL`, `NOTIFICATIONS_HELP_URL`, `cycleTheme`, `HelpIcon`, and `ThemeModeIcon` live in `components/global-chrome.tsx`; `top-bar.tsx` exports none of them, and the footer, the settings dialog, `app.tsx`, and `board-page.tsx` all import from that one module.
**Why**: single definitions are the no-drift requirement, and `top-bar.tsx` uses none of these symbols itself — re-exporting from it would be an orphan seam pointing at a file with no stake in them.
**Rejected**: re-exporting from `top-bar.tsx` (dead indirection); duplicating the SVGs and the cycle step in the sidebar (guaranteed drift, which is the whole thing the move prevents).
*Introduced by*: 260724-6j1v-sidebar-footer-chrome-settings

### Footer version is a readout; the overflow menu's version row stays the update surface
**Decision**: the footer's `v…` line is a passive readout with click-to-copy only, rendering nothing when `daemonVersion` is null. The overflow menu's fixed version row keeps both its copy form and its update/⟳-check forms.
**Why**: the footer's job is resting identity ("what am I running") at a glance; the menu row is the *unified update button's* resting form and owns the check affordance and the update flip (§ Steady-state version surfaces → surface 5). Duplicating the update surface would mean two places to keep the updating-state and toast behavior in sync. Rendering nothing on a null version avoids a dead copy target and a `vundefined` placeholder — the same degrade-or-omit rule every version surface follows.
**Rejected**: a `RunKit` text fallback in the footer when the version is unknown (surface 5's form, but here it would be a live-looking button with nothing to copy); moving the update surface into the footer (loses the menu's ⟳ + attention-badge wiring for no gain).
*Introduced by*: 260724-6j1v-sidebar-footer-chrome-settings

### The settings Notifications row explains an unsupported browser instead of hiding
**Decision**: on push `state === "unsupported"` the settings Notifications row stays present and renders a "Not supported in this browser" note with no action buttons.
**Why**: render-nothing is a bar-chip idiom (an inapplicable chip should not reserve space in a fixed cluster), but a settings pane is an inventory of what exists — a silently missing row reads as a bug, while a stated absence answers the question. Blast radius is one branch of one row.
**Rejected**: hiding the row (carries the chip idiom into a pane where it misleads); a disabled Enable button with no explanation (looks broken without saying why).
*Introduced by*: 260724-6j1v-sidebar-footer-chrome-settings

### Dialog theme controls are self-contained, not the `ThemeSelector` modal
**Decision**: The dialog's theme-pair surface is self-contained — a System/Light/Dark mode control plus two `<select>`s (preferred dark / preferred light) driving the existing `setTheme()` — rather than dispatching the `"theme-selector:open"` event to reuse the top-bar `ThemeSelector`.
**Why**: `ThemeSelector` is mounted only inside `AppShell` (`app.tsx`), so the event has no listener on `/board/$name` — the dialog's core every-page-mount promise. `setTheme(id)` already owns slot updates + the partial-merge POST + localStorage sync, so the dialog reuses that wiring (the intake's second-surface requirement) without the modal. The top-bar selector stays; the dialog is additive.
**Rejected**: moving the `ThemeSelector` mount to `AppLayout` (a behavior change to an unrelated surface, out of scope); live-preview machinery inside the dialog (the selector modal already owns preview UX).
*Introduced by*: 260723-o7q8-settings-dialog

### Edge-docked padding keeps the `max()` shape, but the bottom floor is raised on coarse pointers and gated by a JS keyboard signal
**Decision**: Edge-docked chrome keeps the `max(<floor>, env(safe-area-inset-<edge>))` expression under `viewport-fit=cover`, but the bottom bar's floor is a `--bottom-bar-floor` custom property raised from 6px to 1rem under `@media (pointer: coarse)` on `html:not(.kb-open)` — where `kb-open` is toggled on `<html>` by `useVisualViewport` when the on-screen keyboard opens.
**Why**: `env(safe-area-inset-bottom)` resolves to **0** in in-browser iOS Safari for this `position: fixed`, non-scrollable app (device-confirmed 2026-08-05), so the `max()` arm alone leaves the chips under the corner arc — the clearance has to come from the floor. A floor is static, so keyboard-collapse behavior has to be observed explicitly: iOS support for `interactive-widget=resizes-content` is unreliable, and `env()` carries no keyboard signal here (it reads 0 in both states). Keeping `max()` costs nothing and lets a genuine standalone-PWA inset still win. The JS half stays minimal — one class toggle inside the hook's existing rAF-coalesced pass, no new listeners; all padding math stays in CSS.
**Rejected**: a static coarse floor with no keyboard gate (wastes ~10px above the keyboard on an already-short viewport); treating `env()` collapse or the layout-viewport resize as the keyboard signal (untrue on device — this is the premise the earlier CSS-only design rested on); JS-probing `env()` and substituting a measured value (more machinery, still needs a hardcoded magnitude when the probe reads 0); a PWA manifest / standalone route to make `env()` report real insets (changes the install story to fix a browser-tab bug); a fixed 34px pad (wrong on non-notched phones).
*Introduced by*: 260805-fi9m-bottom-bar-keyboard-aware-safe-floor

### Keyboard-open detection is a width-keyed max-height baseline, not an absolute reference height
**Decision**: `useVisualViewport` treats the un-keyboarded baseline as the maximum `visualViewport.height` observed since the last `visualViewport.width` change, and calls the keyboard open when `height < baseline − 150px` (`KEYBOARD_DELTA_PX`).
**Why**: An on-screen keyboard changes height but never width, while rotations and window/split-screen resizes change width — so a width change is a reliable baseline-reset trigger, and a max-height baseline self-heals after any transient shrink. 150px clears iOS URL-bar chrome deltas (~50–114px) and undercuts any real keyboard (~260px+). The two residual gaps are accepted because both fail toward the pre-change 6px floor rather than a wrong pad: rotating or resizing *while* the keyboard is open re-baselines to the keyboarded height (clearing `kb-open` until the next close/open cycle), and a mount with the keyboard already up reads as closed until it first closes.
**Rejected**: comparing against `window.innerHeight` (browsers honoring `interactive-widget=resizes-content` shrink the layout viewport too, erasing the delta); `screen.height` (orientation semantics differ per platform); a dedicated keyboard-detection hook or extra listener (the existing rAF-coalesced pass already sees every geometry change). A desktop height-only window resize can false-positive, which is harmless — the only consumer is `@media (pointer: coarse)`-scoped.
*Introduced by*: 260805-fi9m-bottom-bar-keyboard-aware-safe-floor

### Shell safe-area gate ships without pinning the native paint mechanism
**Decision**: The top bar's `pt-[env(safe-area-inset-top)]` is gated off with `isShell()` inside the desktop shell without first pinning the exact Electron-native mechanism behind the v3.12.12 band-over-toolbar symptom (regression boundary: PR #487, the accent titlebar strip).
**Why**: Whichever agent paints the band, the SPA reserving the titlebar band twice — `ShellTitlebarStrip` plus safe-area padding — is a defect on its own, so removing the second reservation is correct and sufficient regardless of the rendering internals. Coverage is the unit test driving `isShell()` through its real seam (`window.runkitShell` injection/deletion) plus a manual in-shell check post-release — Playwright cannot cover the shell (`isShell()` is false in e2e); if the band survives a release build, the native-paint investigation reopens as a separate change.
**Rejected**: blocking the fix on a DevTools probe from the affected shell window (the double reservation is a defect independent of the probe's result); `vi.mock("@/lib/shell")` in the test (the bridge-injection pattern exercises the real narrowing seam).
*Introduced by*: 260805-9hn1-shell-safe-area-inset-fix

### One stroke-SVG icon system in sidebar rows, sized by ink not by position
**Decision**: Sidebar row action glyphs are all `sidebar/icons.tsx` stroke SVGs on one idiom (24-unit viewBox, `strokeWidth={2}`, 13px) — `PlusIcon` and `CloseIcon` replace the former text `+`/`✕` — and `BotIcon` is optically centered by a `viewBox` y-shift rather than a wrapper transform.
**Why**: The reported "icons aren't equidistant" was optical, not geometric: measured center-to-center gaps were already even to within 0.7px, but a 16px text glyph beside a 13px stroke SVG differs in ink width and weight, which reads as uneven whitespace. Equalizing the ink is the root-cause fix; nudging positions would have chased the symptom. Shifting `BotIcon`'s viewBox keeps the rendered box exactly 13px, which a `translate-y` wrapper class would not guarantee across the cluster's flex centering.
**Rejected**: nudging gaps/padding per button (treats the symptom); resizing the text glyphs down to 13px (still two ink systems); a `translate-y-[0.5px]` wrapper on the bot button (changes the box, not the drawing).
*Introduced by*: 260724-2bmy-mobile-chrome-polish

### Compose textarea owns its own row at a 2-line default
**Decision**: The compose strip stacks — textarea alone on row 1 at `w-full` and `rows={2}`, buttons on row 2 — with the 2-line default applying on desktop as well as touch.
**Why**: Sharing one row left the input ~52% of a 375px strip while typing space is the surface's entire purpose; a stack buys the full width at the cost of one button-row height, which the strip can afford as docked chrome. The 2-row default is the user's explicit call, and it also removes the desktop oddity where a 1-line textarea rendered 2px *shorter* than the buttons beside it.
**Rejected**: keeping one row and shrinking the buttons (recovers little width, hurts touch targets); a coarse-only two-row layout (divergent desktop/mobile DOM for no benefit — the desktop 1-line default was itself the complaint).
*Introduced by*: 260724-2bmy-mobile-chrome-polish

### Panel toggle joins the bordered-chip family instead of staying borderless
**Decision**: The top-bar hamburger is a bordered chip (`rounded border border-border hover:border-text-secondary`) with a 16px icon, and the brand crumb carries an explicit `min-h` on the same height axis as the button controls.
**Why**: The toggle already shared its neighbors' box, but reading as a *different control family* — borderless with a 20px glyph beside bordered chips with 13–14px glyphs — is what made the row look misaligned; matching the established sibling convention (HistoryNav / `LINK_CRUMB_CLASS`) fixes the perception with one class list, and the border finally gives `rk-glint`'s border-flip something to flip. The brand `min-h` is the mechanical half: the anchor measured 4px shorter than the buttons beside it.
**Rejected**: keeping it borderless and only shrinking the icon (leaves two control families in one cluster); putting the `min-h` inside `LINK_CRUMB_CLASS` (inert on the inline server crumb, which is not a flex box).
*Introduced by*: 260724-2bmy-mobile-chrome-polish

### Terminal link-opening is the SPA's job, via the shared `window.open` idiom
**Decision**: `WebLinksAddon` carries an explicit activation handler calling `window.open(uri, "_blank", "noopener,noreferrer")`; the addon's default handler is not used.
**Why**: The default opens a URL-less blank window and then assigns `location.href`, so an embedding shell only ever sees `about:blank` — the URL is gone before any host-side policy can act on it. No amount of Electron-side work can recover it, which makes this a frontend fix by necessity rather than by preference. Routing through `window.open` also puts terminal links on the same three-arg external-open idiom as every other outbound link in the SPA, so the shell needs exactly one window-open policy rather than a per-affordance special case.
**Rejected**: Fixing it in the desktop shell (impossible — `about:blank` carries no URL); a custom `linkHandler` on the `Terminal` options instead of the addon handler (reimplements the addon's URL detection for the same outcome); a Playwright e2e for the click path (xterm renders links on a canvas, so link-region clicks are not reliably automatable).
*Introduced by*: 260730-e9lz-shell-terminal-links-external

### The action tier's LETTERS are uniform; its modifier is per platform and per host
**Decision**: The canonical letters (N/T/W/H/L/A + [/]//) are the same on every platform, but the modifier is not: Windows/Linux uses `Shift+Ctrl` throughout, while macOS demotes to the unshifted ⌘ tier wherever nothing immovable blocks it — [/]// in both mac hosts, N/T/W inside the desktop shell only, with H/L/A staying shifted everywhere. The refinement is declarative data on each binding (`macTier`/`macShellOnly`) resolved once in `defaultComboFor`.
**Why**: Letter consistency is what must not fragment; chord weight is negotiable per environment, and the constraints that force Shift were never symmetric. Plain Ctrl must stay with the pane on Windows/Linux (Ctrl+W delete-word, Ctrl+L clear, Ctrl+T fzf, Ctrl+N/P history) — a macOS-only constraint-free tier gets taxed for nothing by uniformity, and the desktop shell exists precisely to hand the SPA the unshifted ⌘ tier, so a uniformly-shifted tier left that premise unspent. What blocks a demotion is genuinely immovable, not aesthetic: ⌘H is macOS Hide and ⌘A is select-all / the shell's Edit role, and demoting L alone would split the H/L pair across two tiers — the same fragmentation the letter rule exists to prevent. Because the resolver already funnels every consumer through one effective map, expressing the tier as per-key data costs no architecture.
**Rejected**: A uniform shifted tier on every platform (taxes mac users a three-key chord the environment would give them in two, and leaves the shell's whole reason for existing unused); demoting H/L/A too (⌘H/⌘A are dead in both mac hosts — the chord would silently do nothing); demoting N/T/W on mac *browsers* as well (browser-reserved unshifted AND shifted, so they stay palette-only there); per-platform `DEFAULT_BINDINGS` arrays or a resolver-level actionId→tier table (duplicates every binding, or splits the per-key truth across two structures).
*Introduced by*: 260730-n789-macos-cmd-tier-shortcuts

### The tables are the chord authority; the key map is a discovery and rebind tool
**Decision**: The overlay renders ONE keycap grid with a "Holding ⇧⌘ | ⌘" modifier picker, and the browser-vs-desktop divergence lives on the affected table ROWS (a `desktop` badge + the other host's chord) rather than in a second grid or a per-map caption.
**Why**: Two stacked grids named the registry's internal tier taxonomy in user-facing chrome and re-answered per-action chords the rows already carry, at double the vertical mass — which pushed the authoritative tables and the TMUX section below the fold on short viewports. Giving each surface one job removes the duplication instead of restyling it: the map answers the physical question ("hold this — here's what my keyboard does", the model Karabiner-style keyboard viewers use), and the rows carry the per-action truth including where it differs by host. The divergence is genuinely per-binding — exactly the `macTier`+`macShellOnly` trio — so a row is where the fact belongs.
**Rejected**: Keeping both grids with de-jargonized headers (still double the vertical mass, still duplicates the tables); labelling the whole ⌘ layer desktop-only (factually wrong — ⌘K/⌘./⌘[/⌘]//⌘/ all work in a browser tab; only the trio differs); a registry change to carry the new presentation (unnecessary — `macTier`/`macShellOnly` already ride onto `EffectiveBinding`, and `defaultComboFor` already computes the other-host chord).
*Introduced by*: 260801-r8j2-shortcuts-map-modifier-picker

### The picker IS the map label, and the inactive layer falls back by derivation
**Decision**: The map header's left slot is "Holding" plus the segmented control itself (the selected segment supplies the modifier caps), with a static "Holding **Shift Ctrl**" on the Win·Linux display; the rendered layer is derived (`displayPlatform === "mac" ? mapTier : "shifted"`) rather than reset when the display toggles.
**Why**: The picker and the layer label are the same header element — rendering them separately would duplicate the modifier caps and widen the header row, and a one-option segmented control on Win·Linux is dead chrome. Deriving the fallback matches "the option disappears" rather than "the selection is destroyed": a user flipping the display to Win·Linux and back gets their ⌘ selection returned, consistent with the session-scoped view state the fold already establishes.
**Rejected**: A separate "Holding ⇧⌘" title plus a detached picker (duplicates the caps); resetting `mapTier` on display change (loses the selection on a round-trip for no benefit); persisting the selection to localStorage (a reading preference within one sheet, not a device setting).
*Introduced by*: 260801-r8j2-shortcuts-map-modifier-picker

### Chord handlers are the palette action bodies, looked up by id
**Decision**: The AppShell dispatcher resolves `create-session`, `create-window`, `kill-window`, `agent-next-waiting`, `go-back`, `go-forward`, and `settings-open` by finding the palette action with the matching id and reusing its `onSelect`.
**Why**: `actionId` doubles as the palette id by design, so the lookup is free — and reuse makes chord/palette drift impossible, including gating: a palette entry that does not exist (e.g. `kill-window` with no active session) yields no handler, and the dispatcher's no-handler fall-through is exactly the desired no-op.
**Rejected**: A parallel handler implementation per chord — duplicates every gate and drifts from the palette behavior it must mirror.
*Introduced by*: 260730-g40a-keyboard-shortcut-registry-overlay

### Enablement-bearing chords keep their own listeners; the registry owns definition and suppression
**Decision**: ⌘K, ⌘\, ⌘., and `` Ctrl+` `` dispatch from component/hook-local listeners that read their combo from the effective map and gate on the shared suppression predicate; only the board pane-cycle runs through `useKeybindingDispatch`.
**Why**: Centralizing *definition* and *suppression* is the registry's charter; centralizing *enablement* is not — palette open-state, chat capability, and lens availability are local state, and routing them through one dispatcher would need context plumbing for zero user-visible gain.
**Rejected**: One dispatcher for every chord (high regression risk, new context plumbing); leaving these four out of the registry entirely (they would be unrebindable and invisible to the overlay).
*Introduced by*: 260730-g40a-keyboard-shortcut-registry-overlay

### The terminal seam refuses shifted chords everywhere and cmd chords only on mac, metaKey-gated
**Decision**: `shouldRefuseTerminalChord` returns `true` for an enabled shifted-tier match on any platform, plus an enabled cmd-tier match on macOS **only when `event.metaKey` is set**; everything else returns `true` from the handler (reaches the pane), after the ⌘C copy case.
**Why**: Refusal must be exactly as wide as the set of chords the pane cannot use. Legacy TTY encoding cannot distinguish Ctrl+Shift+letter from Ctrl+letter, so the shifted tier costs the pane nothing anywhere; ⌘ chords never reach a pane as control bytes, so refusing them on mac is loss-free and is what lets the demoted ⌘[/⌘]/⌘/ fire under terminal focus. The `metaKey` gate is the load-bearing half: `matchesCombo`'s cmd tier accepts Meta **or** Ctrl, so an ungated refusal would steal Ctrl+[ (ESC) from mac panes — the same hazard that forbids cmd refusal on Windows/Linux, where CmdOrCtrl *is* the pane's own Ctrl. Gating on the platform + `metaKey` therefore keeps the Win/Linux seam byte-identical rather than making it a special case.
**Rejected**: Refusing every registry match (breaks plain-Ctrl pane chords everywhere); refusing all cmd-tier matches on mac ungated (breaks Ctrl+[ = ESC); narrowing `matchesCombo`'s cmd tier to Meta-only on mac (would change the legacy ⌘K/⌘\/⌘. predicates project-wide); adding a `preventDefault` in the handler (xterm's non-preventing refusal is what lets the event bubble to the window dispatcher). Residual: on Windows/Linux an action deliberately rebound *into* the cmd/ctrl tier is not refused and reaches the pane while the terminal has focus.
*Introduced by*: 260730-n789-macos-cmd-tier-shortcuts

### Overrides are localStorage diffs with single-press steal-and-warn
**Decision**: Only differences from the defaults are persisted (`{ [actionId]: {code,tier} | null }`), and one capture press hands the combo over, unbinding the previous owner and flagging it.
**Why**: A diff blob keeps the shipped defaults authoritative — adding or renaming a default binding needs no migration, and re-capturing an action's own **host** default drops its entry rather than freezing a redundant one. Single-press steal keeps rebinding to one gesture and is honest about the consequence, since the victim's `unbound` state is visible in the overlay until it is rebound or reset. Storing only diffs also makes the per-platform tiers free: overrides are per-device by construction, so a device's platform is implicit and no per-platform storage key or migration exists. localStorage matches Constitution II (no database) and the `runkit-terminal-font-size` precedent.
**Rejected**: Persisting the full effective map (defaults freeze into user storage, and the per-platform default would be baked in); a press-again-to-confirm steal (two gestures for one intent, and the warning already carries the information); a backend-stored per-user map (a new persistence surface for a per-device preference); a per-platform override shape (per-device storage already makes per-platform inherent).
*Introduced by*: 260730-g40a-keyboard-shortcut-registry-overlay

### A same-combo global↔scoped pair is precedence for conflicts but still a steal target for capture
**Decision**: `findMatches` orders matches non-global-first and the dispatcher fires the first one with a handler; `findConflicts` flags only **equal**-scope pairs; `applyCapture`'s victim search stays `scopesOverlap`-wide, and its own-host-default check short-circuits **before** that search.
**Why**: Once dispatch is scoped-beats-global, a global↔scoped shadow leaves both bindings fully functional (the scoped one wins on its route, the global one everywhere else), so reporting it as a defaults-integrity conflict would make the shipped mac map permanently "dirty" — the board ⌘[/⌘] pair shadowing back/forward is intended, not a defect. Capture stealing cannot be narrowed the same way: four chords dispatch through component-local listeners that never see `findMatches` precedence, so a cross-scope rebind onto their combos would genuinely double-fire, and steal-with-warning is the visible, reversible guard. The short-circuit ordering follows from the same shape — the mac defaults ship shadow pairs, so a victim search running first would let a *no-op* re-capture of one partner unbind the other.
**Rejected**: Treating the shadow as a conflict (would flag the shipped mac defaults); narrowing `applyCapture` to equal scopes too (silently allows double-fire on the locally-listened chords); a schema flag marking locally-listened bindings (scope creep for one edge); resolving the shared combo by moving the board pane-cycle off ⌘[/⌘] (breaks an established board chord to accommodate a global default).
*Introduced by*: 260730-n789-macos-cmd-tier-shortcuts

### Macro targets are riff presets and palette action ids, never shell strings
**Decision**: `MacroTarget` is a two-arm union — a riff **preset name** or a **palette action id**. Execution reaches process spawn only through the existing `POST /api/riff`, which resolves the name as a map-key lookup against `fab/project/config.yaml`-defined presets; an unknown name is a `ValidationErr` → 400.
**Why**: A shortcut executing user-typed shell text would make a web-reachable endpoint run arbitrary commands — precisely what Constitution I exists to prevent. Both motivating flows ("spawn a worktree with a fab-discuss agent", "spawn a codex loop") are already expressible as riff presets, so the macro tier adds **zero** new process-execution surface: it is a keyboard route to an already-validated spawn, and a localStorage-planted preset name can never reach argv. Arguments belong in the preset definition, inside the committed-config trust boundary.
**Rejected**: An arbitrary command string on the macro (Constitution I); an `args?: string[]` passthrough on the riff target (`POST /api/riff` has no args seam and its `task` replaces preset panes, so it needs a backend extension the "no new exec surface" premise rules out — additive to the union later if that seam appears); a preset-authoring UI in the web app (Constitution IV — presets already have a home and an authoring path in fabconfig).
*Introduced by*: 260730-hbyh-shortcut-macro-riff-bindings

### Macro combos live in the keybindings diff map, not the macro definition
**Decision**: A macro definition carries no combo; its binding is an ordinary `runkit-keybindings` override entry keyed by the macro's actionId, and a macro without an entry resolves unbound (`disabledReason: "user"`).
**Why**: One binding store and one resolution path means steal-with-warning, per-row reset, reset-all, browser-reserved disabling, palette hints, the overlay key map, and the terminal seam all work on macros with the existing machinery — no parallel plumbing, and reusing the steal-victim state gives the overlay's `unbound` affordance for free.
**Rejected**: Storing the combo inside the MacroAction — a second combo store that `applyCapture`/steal/reset could not see without duplicated wiring.
*Introduced by*: 260730-hbyh-shortcut-macro-riff-bindings

### Keyless defaults (`code: ""`) instead of a macro-specific resolver
**Decision**: `resolveBindings` carries one extra rule — an effective combo with an empty `code` resolves unbound — and macros enter resolution as ordinary defaults via `macroToBinding`.
**Why**: One resolver keeps every consumer (dispatch, hints, overlay, terminal seam, capture) macro-agnostic; builtins always carry a code, so they are untouched by the rule.
**Rejected**: A parallel `resolveMacroBindings` merged after the fact — duplicates the override/reserved/steal logic and forks the effective-map contract.
*Introduced by*: 260730-hbyh-shortcut-macro-riff-bindings

### An unrepresentable shell claim is recorded as absence plus the system claim that replaces it
**Decision**: The mac Hosts-switcher claim is expressed by *narrowing* `SHELL_SWITCHER_DIGITS` to `platform: "other"` and adding mac `owner: "system"` ⇧⌘3/4/5 "screenshot" rows — no Option tier is added to `BindingTier`, and the freed mac ⇧⌘1/2/6–9 carry no rows at all. The overlay's decorative digit-run ellipsis cell keys its claimed styling off `Digit3`.
**Why**: The mac accelerator lives on ⌥⌘, which the registry cannot express because Alt is excluded from every tier — and that exclusion is precisely why ⌥⌘ was chosen ([desktop-shell](/run-kit/desktop-shell.md) § Design Decisions → The mac shell tier is ⌥⌘). Adding a tier to represent the claim would undo the property that makes the key safe. What the key map still owes the user is honesty about what a mac ⇧⌘ digit press actually does, which the system screenshot rows supply: shell and system claims never disable a binding (only `browser` claims reserve), so this is pure display data and `resolveBindings` behavior is untouched — a capture onto mac ⇧⌘3/4/5 now warns "claimed by system (screenshot)" instead of "claimed by shell". `Digit3` is the ellipsis key because it is the only digit in the run claimed on **both** displays, so mid-run claims stay visible where `Digit1` would render the mac trio invisible.
**Rejected**: A fourth `BindingTier` for Option-carrying chords (destroys the un-claimable property the accelerator choice rests on, and `captureFromEvent` rejects Alt chords by design); leaving the mac shifted digits claim-free (the key map would show nine free keys where three take screenshots); claiming the freed ⇧⌘1/2/6–9 defensively (they genuinely are page real estate — a claim there would lock keys nothing owns).
*Introduced by*: 260731-nv5r-mac-host-switcher-alt-cmd

### Shared top-bar button token is hosted beside `MENU_ROW_*`
**Decision**: The `TOP_BAR_BUTTON*` / `TOP_BAR_BUTTON_H` / `TOP_BAR_SEGMENT_H` constants live in `top-bar-overflow-menu.tsx` next to `MENU_ROW_*`, and the square token is a FIXED size (`w-[28px] h-[28px] coarse:w-[30px] coarse:h-[30px]`), not a `min-*` floor.
**Why**: Every consumer — `top-bar.tsx`, `open-button.tsx`, `view-switcher.tsx`, the menu itself — already imports that file, so the same no-cycle host precedent `MENU_ROW_*` established applies with no new module. The fixed size addresses the root cause of visible size inconsistency in the cluster: a `min-*` floor lets each box stretch to its own content, so a bare-glyph control renders smaller than a padded sibling and the class string drifts further with every copy-paste. Fixing the size once, in a shared constant, makes that class of defect unrepresentable.
**Rejected**: A new `top-bar-button.ts` module (an extra file for a handful of string constants when an established host exists); keeping `min-*` floors and normalizing per callsite (treats the symptom — the drift returns with the next copy-paste).
*Introduced by*: 260731-oiho-navbar-consolidation-menu-density

### The split ▾ lists both directions; the primary stays a fixed direction
**Decision**: The merged `SplitControl`'s ▾ dropdown lists BOTH `Split horizontal` and `Split vertical`; the primary segment always performs one fixed direction, never last-used.
**Why**: Split-button convention (and the in-bar `OpenButton` precedent) puts the complete option set in the dropdown, so a one-row menu would hide half the control's capability behind the assumption that the user knows the primary's direction. A fixed primary keeps the primary click deterministic — the same guarantee the two per-direction buttons gave.
**Rejected**: A single-row dropdown carrying only the non-default direction (hides the complete option set); a last-used-tracking primary (makes the most-used affordance in the cluster non-deterministic).
*Introduced by*: 260731-oiho-navbar-consolidation-menu-density

### Horizontal is the default split direction, and one direction vocabulary spans every surface
**Decision**: The primary segment's fixed direction is HORIZONTAL (side-by-side, tmux `-h`), and horizontal leads wherever both directions appear — the ▾ menu, the overflow `SplitMenuRow` pair, and both command palettes. Every surface names directions after tmux's own flags: horizontal = `horizontal: true` = `split-window -h` = beside; vertical = `horizontal: false` = `split-window -v` = stacked. The terminal palette (`app.tsx`), the board palette (`board-page.tsx`), and the `/api/keybindings` whitelist labels are aligned to that vocabulary; the backend `horizontal` boolean contract is untouched.
**Why**: Side-by-side is the dominant working layout, and a split-button's default and its first menu row must agree — so the ordering rides with the default rather than moving on its own. The vocabulary is settled in the same decision because two naming schemes for one action let the same word mean opposite things: a chip labeled "Split horizontal" running `-h` beside a palette entry labeled `Split Horizontal` running `-v` means a user who learns the word from one surface gets the opposite split from the other. The chip's mapping is the internally consistent one — its glyph, its label, and tmux's flag name all agree — which makes it the convention to standardize on rather than the one to relabel.
**Rejected**: Reordering the menus while leaving the primary vertical (the default and the first row would disagree, breaking the split-button convention the control rests on); relabeling the chip to match the palettes (puts the label at odds with both its glyph and `split-window -h`); leaving the palette booleans inverted and documenting the divergence (a same-name-opposite-action bug is not documentable away); persisting a last-used direction (the primary is deliberately deterministic — § above).
*Introduced by*: 260806-2x2h-split-default-horizontal-first

### Menu sections are named on the registry entry, not derived in the menu
**Decision**: Each `RegistryEntry` carries `menuGroup: "view" | "window" | "app"` (surfaced as `group` on `OverflowMenuRow`); `TopBarOverflowMenu` partitions rows by group and renders non-empty sections in the fixed View → Window → App order.
**Why**: The partition preserves registry (pyramid) order *within* each section for free, so the `View:` switcher rows keep leading the menu with zero re-sorting, and a new control declares its section at the one place it is already declaring `modes`/`hidden`/`menuOnly`.
**Rejected**: Grouping inside the menu component by hardcoded id lists (silently drifts the moment the registry gains or renames an entry); sorting rows into groups by tier (tier encodes drop priority, not conceptual grouping — the two answer different questions).
*Introduced by*: 260731-oiho-navbar-consolidation-menu-density

### Demoted in-bar components stay intact rather than being deleted
**Decision**: `ClosePaneButton`, `FixedWidthToggle`, `TerminalFontControl`, and the `ViewSwitcher` pill remain in the codebase — reachable only through their registry entries' `barRender`, which `menuOnly` never calls — instead of being removed with their demotions.
**Why**: The revert contract is what makes a demotion cheap to try: reverting is deleting one flag, and that only holds while the bar form still compiles and renders. The four components are tracked as deletion candidates for a later change that confirms the demotions stick, so the cleanup is a deliberate follow-up rather than an omission.
**Rejected**: Deleting each component with its demotion (turns a one-flag revert into a re-implementation, and would lose the `TerminalFontControl` popover's `Reset terminal font` stepper — the menu row has `−`/`+` only — before its replacement is agreed).
*Introduced by*: 260731-oiho-navbar-consolidation-menu-density

### One shortcuts surface with one filter, not two dialogs or tabs
**Decision**: The registry overlay is the only shortcuts dialog. tmux keybindings are a read-only section inside it, and there are no tabs — a single filter and a single scroll span app rows, macros, and tmux rows, with a sticky jump-nav chip row (live per-section match counts while filtering) standing in for tab navigation.
**Why**: The user's query is "what's the key for X?", and answering it should not require first knowing whether X belongs to the app or to tmux. Two dialogs made the answer depend on that knowledge, and the second dialog's hardcoded App rows drifted stale the moment the registry gained tiers and per-device overrides — the registry surface is self-updating by construction. Tabs would have re-imposed the same "pick the right pane first" cost inside one dialog; the tmux set is small enough that one scroll stays readable, and the chip counts keep hits below the fold discoverable.
**Rejected**: Keeping both dialogs (the staleness and the which-one-do-I-open question are structural, not a copy problem); tabs inside the merged overlay (reintroduces the pre-selection cost the merge exists to remove); making tmux rows rebindable (tmux keys are pressed inside the pane and configured in `configs/tmux/default.conf` — run-kit only documents them).
*Introduced by*: 260801-sm6g-keyboard-shortcuts-consolidation

### The compose strip focuses on open, and only on open
**Decision**: The strip focuses its textarea on the off→on transition, via a module-level focus-on-open flag marked by `toggleComposeStrip` and consumed-and-cleared by the mount effect. After-send, Escape, and route remounts keep the no-steal rule.
**Why**: In practice every open was followed by a manual click into the textarea — opening the strip *is* the intent to type, so the blanket no-steal rule cost a gesture on the one transition where focus was unambiguous. Routing the flag through the single `toggleComposeStrip` seam covers every open path (chip, palette, chord, drag-drop, board twin) without per-affordance wiring, and keys the behavior on the TRANSITION rather than the mount — which is exactly what keeps a route remount with the strip already enabled from stealing focus. Consuming unconditionally (even when the effect declines in the "no target" state) is what stops a stale flag surfacing as a surprise focus grab later.
**Rejected**: Focusing on every mount (steals focus on terminal↔board remounts); a `focusOnOpen` prop threaded to both footer mounts (two wiring sites for one module-level fact, and the drag-drop auto-enable path bypasses them both); calling the existing `focusComposeStrip()` registry from the toggle (the strip is not mounted yet at toggle time — the flag exists precisely to bridge that gap).
*Introduced by*: 260801-sm6g-keyboard-shortcuts-consolidation

### A compose draft belongs to its send target, not to the URL and not to the user
**Decision**: Drafts are keyed by `entryKey(server, windowId)` — the strip's live send target — so each window owns its unsent text and attachments, and focus changes swap the visible draft rather than carrying one draft along.
**Why**: The strip's target is already resolved live at send time (§ Live target), so binding the draft to that same identity is what makes the `→ {window}` label truthful: what you see aimed at a window is what that window will receive. It also makes a cross-worktree attachment structurally impossible — the attachment and the worktree it was uploaded to are two halves of one keyed entry — which is what allows the entire re-home path to be deleted rather than hardened. Keying survives window renames, since `windowId` is tmux-stable, and the same draft appears whether the window is reached via its terminal route or a board pane.
**Rejected**: A single global draft carried across navigation — showing a draft composed for A while B is targeted is a wrong-pane-send footgun that the target label only partially mitigates, and it is the sole reason attachment re-homing had to exist. Keying by URL — equivalent to target keying on terminal routes, but on a board it pins one draft on screen while pane focus cycles underneath, recreating exactly the wrong-pane risk it was meant to remove.
*Introduced by*: 260801-cyth-per-target-persistent-compose-drafts

### Draft text is persisted synchronously; attachments are not persisted at all
**Decision**: Every commit writes the text map through to `localStorage` synchronously (no debounce), and `File` objects are never persisted — a hydrated draft carries text only.
**Why**: Drafts are a few kilobytes at most, so the I/O saved by a debounce is negligible next to what it costs — a flush-on-unload edge case and non-determinism in unit and e2e tests — and the synchronous write closes the lose-a-keystroke-on-instant-reload window that motivated persistence in the first place. Dropping attachments is not a compromise but the honest boundary of the storage: uploads are eager and their path lines live in the persisted text, so the only casualty of a refresh is the preview thumbnails, while the files themselves are already on the target worktree's disk and still send correctly.
**Rejected**: Debounced writes (see above). IndexedDB `File` persistence — real complexity and a second storage substrate to buy back thumbnails. `sessionStorage` — dies with the tab, which fails the browser-restart case the persistence exists for. A `storage`-event multi-tab sync — two tabs composing to the same window is rare enough that last-write-wins is the right trade.
*Introduced by*: 260801-cyth-per-target-persistent-compose-drafts

### One global listener set over per-key snapshots, and one prune pipeline over both persistence paths
**Decision**: The keyed store keeps a single listener set (any commit notifies every subscriber) with per-key stable snapshot objects, and both the write path and the hydrate path call the same `pruneDraftEntries()` helper.
**Why**: At most one strip is mounted at a time, so per-key subscription channels would add bookkeeping for zero benefit; what `useSyncExternalStore` actually needs is snapshot **identity** stability per key, which the snapshot cache provides. Sharing the prune helper matters because the two paths must agree by construction — a divergence between what is written and what is accepted back would let an entry the writer rejects survive a reload, or an entry the reader drops be silently rewritten. The age check is deliberately two-sided so a future-dated `updatedAt` cannot sort to the front and hold a cap slot forever.
**Rejected**: Per-key listener channels (complexity for a single-subscriber store). Separate filter/sort/slice pipelines at each site (the duplication that a review caught — two copies of a policy that must never disagree). A `__resetForTest` private export for the test seam — exporting the real `hydrateComposeDrafts()` instead means the tests reset through the same code path production uses, so a reset can never drift from a real hydration.
*Introduced by*: 260801-cyth-per-target-persistent-compose-drafts

### A chord may name a behavior even when it cannot name a target
**Decision**: `open-last-used` (⇧⌘O) is a builtin binding, while the per-target `Open: <label>` palette entries stay chord-less.
**Why**: The objection to chording Open was that targets are data-driven — the set varies per deployment, so no static chord can name one. "Re-run the last-used target" is a behavior, not an app, so it is nameable and stable across deployments; it also gives the split-button's primary segment the keyboard twin Constitution V wants. The empty case degrades to a toast rather than a popped menu, because a chord has no anchor to pop a mouse menu from.
**Rejected**: Per-target chords (unnameable, and they would collide differently on every deployment); leaving the primary segment keyboard-unreachable (the most-used Open path had no chord at all).
*Introduced by*: 260801-sm6g-keyboard-shortcuts-consolidation

### A tooltip keycap chip is resolved from the registry, never hard-coded
**Decision**: A `Tip`'s `kbd` slot for a registry-bound action is derived at the call site from the effective map (`useKeybindings()` → `byAction.get(id)` → `formatCombo(…, host.platform)` when `enabled`, else `undefined`), and the chip is **omitted** when the binding is unbound, disabled, or browser-reserved. Non-registry `kbd` strings survive only for chords the registry does not own (the compose/chat submit plus the per-surface Insert keys, `Enter` on the strip and `Alt+Enter` in chat — the submit chip still resolves per-platform through the shared `composeSubmitKeycap()` rather than a literal). The ⌘K chip's button FACE keeps its brand glyph regardless.
**Why**: A hard-coded chip is wrong the moment the platform or the user's override differs from the author's machine — a literal `"⌘K"` reads wrong on every Windows and Linux host and survives a rebind unchanged. Deriving it makes hover-discovery teach the chord that actually works (Constitution V), and omitting rather than dimming a dead chord follows the same rule the palette hints and the overlay's header chord apply: advertising a chord that does nothing is worse than advertising none. The derivation stays a 4-line snippet per site rather than a hook because `withShortcutHints` already covers the palette-array shape and a second abstraction for four scalar call sites would be the heavier option.
**Rejected**: A static string per call site for a registry-bound action (lies under rebinds and off-platform); rendering a dimmed/struck chip for a disabled binding (still advertises the chord, and the tip is one line with no room to explain why it is dead); a shared `useActionChord(id)` hook (four call sites and one shared derivation shape do not yet justify a hook — revisit if the count grows).
*Introduced by*: 260801-mqim-tooltip-shortcut-chips-settings-binding

### `settings-open` takes the shifted tier in browsers and ⌘, only in the shell
**Decision**: `settings-open` ships `code: "Comma"`, base `tier: "shifted"`, with `macTier: "cmd"` + `macShellOnly: true`, and ⌘, is added to `MAC_BROWSER_CMD_CLAIMS` as a browser-owned Preferences claim.
**Why**: ⌘, is the macOS convention for preferences, and the desktop shell binds no accelerator on it — so inside the shell the conventional chord is simply available. A mac *browser* owns ⌘, for its own Preferences, so the browser default has to sit one tier up; recording that as claims data (rather than only as a comment) is what makes a user override onto ⌘, resolve `reserved` in a browser instead of silently advertising a dead chord, while the identical combo stays the enabled default in the shell. Open-only semantics (not toggle) match every macOS app: ⌘, never closes preferences, and `openSettings` being `setIsOpen(true)` makes a re-fire a React state bailout for free.
**Rejected**: A single ⇧⌘, default everywhere (gives up the OS convention in the one host where it is free); an unshifted ⌘, default in browsers too (dead chord — the browser consumes it first); toggle semantics on re-fire (contradicts the macOS convention and the action's own `settings-open` / `Settings: Open` open-vocabulary naming); a `Comma` cell in the overlay's tier-map grids (the `Period`/`Backslash`/`Backquote` precedent leaves punctuation off the fixed grid).
*Introduced by*: 260801-mqim-tooltip-shortcut-chips-settings-binding

### Enter transmits a line in the strip, composes in chat — one classifier, deliberately divergent per surface
**Decision**: `classifyComposeEnter(key, surface)` takes a REQUIRED `surface: "strip" | "chat"` and returns the per-surface plain-Enter action: `"insert-line"` on the strip (transmit `text + "\n"`, clear the draft; `enterKeyHint="send"`) and `"default"` (local newline; `enterKeyHint="enter"`) in chat. Everything else is shared and identical on both surfaces — Shift+Enter = newline, Cmd/Ctrl+Enter = the only submit chord, Alt+Enter = byte-exact insert-without-submit, IME-composing Enter never intercepted. The strip additionally sends a bare `"\r"` on an empty/whitespace-only submit; chat's empty send stays a no-op. The classifier still reads no pointer input.
**Why**: The strip overlays the VISIBLE terminal, so a transmitted line lands in the pane's own composer where the user watches it appear — Claude Code treats a raw `"\n"` as newline-insert, so consecutive Enters stage sentence-per-line exactly like typing into the pane, and one Cmd/Ctrl+Enter (or an empty one, the bare `"\r"`) submits. That stage-then-submit loop is the terminal-faithful flow the surface exists to offer, and gating every staged line behind the obscure Alt+Enter chord — with no keyboard way to press Enter afterwards — made the strip a local drafting box instead. The chat lens cannot show the pane's input box, so the same Enter there would make typed text visibly vanish into an invisible target; its premature-send hazard is also the original one (a reflexive Enter firing a half-written prompt at a live agent). Divergence is therefore the correct answer, and putting it INSIDE the classifier — with `surface` required, no default — keeps one authority for both policies: a call site can choose which policy it gets but cannot invent a third, and a new surface must state its choice rather than silently inherit one. Pointer-independence survives because the divergence axis is surface visibility, not input hardware.
**Rejected**: Keeping one shared policy across both surfaces (sacrifices either the strip's terminal-faithfulness or chat's visibility safety — the two surfaces genuinely differ); a default value for `surface` (recreates exactly the silent drift the shared classifier exists to prevent); branching plain Enter at the call sites (the same drift, one layer down); a user preference toggle (a second policy path for a decision with one defensible default per surface); guarding the shell-pane case where the transmitted `"\n"` executes the line (that IS what Enter does in a terminal — terminal-conventional, documented in a code comment).
*Introduced by*: 260802-lj98-compose-enter-insert-line

### Intercept only the readline chords the platform leaves unbound
**Decision**: The shared readline layer claims exactly Ctrl+U, Ctrl+W, Alt+B, Alt+F, Alt+D — matched on `KeyboardEvent.code` with exact modifiers — and leaves every natively-bound Cocoa editing chord alone. Deletions route through `execCommand("delete")` with a controlled-component-safe fallback; an empty computed range consumes the chord without editing.
**Why**: macOS already implements Ctrl+A/E/B/F/P/N/D/H/K/T/O/Y correctly *and* wires them into the system kill buffer, so reimplementing any of them (Ctrl+K especially) would break its pairing with Ctrl+Y — the five claimed chords are precisely the muscle-memory gap. Matching on `code` is mandatory rather than stylistic: macOS composes `∫`/`ƒ`/`∂` into `key` for Alt+B/F/D, so a key-based matcher would miss the exact chords the layer exists for. `execCommand` is deprecated but the only programmatic edit path the browser records on the native undo stack, and Cmd+Z after a Ctrl+W is table stakes for an editing chord. The empty-range guard is a real corruption fix, not defensiveness — `execCommand("delete")` on a collapsed selection deletes a character backward, so Ctrl+U at line start would silently eat text.
**Rejected**: A full readline emulation incl. a kill ring (duplicates working native behavior and cannot feed the system yank buffer); React `setText(...)` for the deletions (breaks native undo); `key`-based matching (blind to macOS Alt composition); polyfilling Ctrl+W on Windows/Linux browsers (browser-reserved — uninterceptable by a page; recorded as a caveat, with macOS and the desktop shell covered).
*Introduced by*: 260801-hsxm-compose-enter-policy-readline-keys

### Brand logo motion is state-based (segment illumination), not a transform spin
**Decision**: The brand crumb's logo hover is a JS-driven white glow "detach, orbit, land" sweep over the six border segments' fills — no geometry moves, no CSS keyframes, and no `prefers-reduced-motion` gate line (the JS skips itself).
